Tesla confirmed the fire began in the battery pack and was caused by the impact of an object.[&#8202;](#ref-back-522-0)[\[521\]](#refnote-522) As a result of this and other incidents, Tesla announced its decision to extend its current vehicle warranty to cover fire damage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-523-0)[\[522\]](#refnote-523) In March 2014, the NHTSA announced that it had closed the investigation into whether the Model S was prone to catch fire, after Tesla said it would provide more protection to its battery packs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-524-0)[\[523\]](#refnote-524) All Model S cars manufactured after March 6, 2014, have had the 0.25-inch (6.4mm) aluminum shield over the battery pack replaced with a new three-layer shield.[&#8202;](#ref-back-525-0)[\[524\]](#refnote-525) In October 2019, the NHTSA opened an investigation into possible battery defects in Tesla's Model S and X vehicles from 2012 to 2019 that could cause "non-crash" fires.[&#8202;](#ref-back-526-0)[\[525\]](#refnote-526)[&#8202;](#ref-back-527-0)[\[526\]](#refnote-527)[&#8202;](#ref-back-528-0)[\[527\]](#refnote-528)

### Autopilot crashes

See also: [List of Tesla Autopilot crashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Tesla_Autopilot_crashes "List of Tesla Autopilot crashes")

A Model S driver died in a collision with a tractor-trailer in 2016, while the vehicle was in Autopilot mode; the driver is believed to be the first person to have died in a Tesla vehicle in Autopilot.[&#8202;](#ref-back-529-0)[\[528\]](#refnote-529)[&#8202;](#ref-back-530-0)[\[529\]](#refnote-530) The NHTSA investigated the accident but found no safety-related defect trend.[&#8202;](#ref-back-531-0)[\[530\]](#refnote-531) In March 2018, a driver of a Tesla Model X was killed in a crash. Investigators say that the driver of the vehicle had his car in 'self-driving' mode and was using his phone to play games when the vehicle collided with the barrier in the middle of the freeway. Through investigation, the NTSB found that the Tesla malfunctioned due to the system being confused by an exit on the freeway.[&#8202;](#ref-back-532-0)[\[531\]](#refnote-532)

According to a document released in June 2021, the NHTSA has initiated at least 30 investigations into Tesla crashes that were believed to involve the use of Autopilot, with some [involving fatalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_Autopilot#Fatal_crashes "Tesla Autopilot").[&#8202;](#ref-back-533-0)[\[532\]](#refnote-533)[&#8202;](#ref-back-534-0)[\[533\]](#refnote-534) In early September 2021, the NHTSA updated the list with an additional fatality incident[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[534\]](#refnote-auto) and ordered Tesla to hand over all extensive\[ _[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] data pertaining to US cars with Autopilot to determine if there is a safety defect that leads Tesla cars to collide with first-responder vehicles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-1)[\[534\]](#refnote-auto)[&#8202;](#ref-back-536-0)[\[535\]](#refnote-536)[&#8202;](#ref-back-537-0)[\[536\]](#refnote-537) In late September 2021, Tesla released an over-the-air software update to detect emergency lights at night.[&#8202;](#ref-back-538-0)[\[537\]](#refnote-538) In October 2021, the NHTSA asked Tesla why it did not issue a recall when it sent out that update.[&#8202;](#ref-back-539-0)[\[538\]](#refnote-539) In June 2022, the NHTSA said it would expand its probe, extending it to 830,000 cars from all current Tesla models. The probe was moved up from the _Preliminary Evaluation_ level to _Engineering Analysis_. The regulator cited the reason for the expansion as the need to "explore the degree to which Autopilot and associated Tesla systems may exacerbate human factors or behavioral safety risks by undermining the effectiveness of the driver's supervision."[&#8202;](#ref-back-540-0)[\[539\]](#refnote-540)

A safety test conducted by the Dawn Project in August 2022 demonstrated that a test driver using the beta version of Full Self-Driving repeatedly hit a child-sized mannequin in its path,[&#8202;](#ref-back-541-0)[\[540\]](#refnote-541) but there has been controversy over its conclusions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-542-0)[\[541\]](#refnote-542) Several Tesla owners responded by conducting their own, independent tests using children; NHTSA released a statement warning against the practice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-543-0)[\[542\]](#refnote-543)

In 2025, the California DMV filed a lawsuit against Tesla, claiming it had misled drivers about its vehicles' self-driving capabilities. The DMV sought to suspend Tesla's sales and manufacturing in the state of California for a minimum of 30 days.[&#8202;](#ref-back-544-0)[\[543\]](#refnote-544)[&#8202;](#ref-back-545-0)[\[544\]](#refnote-545)

### Software hacking

In August 2015, two researchers said they were able to take control of a Tesla Model S by hacking into the car's entertainment system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-546-0)[\[545\]](#refnote-546) The hack required the researchers to physically access the car.[&#8202;](#ref-back-547-0)[\[546\]](#refnote-547) Tesla issued a [security update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patch_(computing) "Patch (computing)") for the Model S the day after the exploit was announced.[&#8202;](#ref-back-548-0)[\[547\]](#refnote-548)

In September 2016, researchers at [Tencent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tencent "Tencent")'s Keen Security Lab demonstrated a remote attack on a Tesla Model S and controlled the vehicle in both Parking and Driving Mode without physical access. They were able to compromise the automotive networking bus ( [CAN bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CAN_bus "CAN bus")) when the vehicle's web browser was used while the vehicle was connected to a malicious Wi-Fi hotspot.[&#8202;](#ref-back-549-0)[\[548\]](#refnote-549) This was the first case of a remote control exploit demonstrated on a Tesla. The vulnerability was disclosed to Tesla under its [bug bounty program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bug_bounty_program "Bug bounty program") and patched within 10 days, before the exploit was made public.[&#8202;](#ref-back-550-0)[\[549\]](#refnote-550) Tencent also hacked the doors of a Model X in 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-551-0)[\[550\]](#refnote-551)

In January 2018, security researchers informed Tesla that an [Amazon Web Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_Web_Services "Amazon Web Services") account of theirs could be accessed directly from the Internet and that the account had been exploited for [cryptocurrency mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptocurrency_mining "Cryptocurrency mining"). Tesla responded by securing the compromised system, rewarding the security researchers financially via its bug bounty program, and stating that the compromise did not violate customer privacy, nor vehicle safety or security.[&#8202;](#ref-back-552-0)[\[551\]](#refnote-552)[&#8202;](#ref-back-553-0)[\[552\]](#refnote-553) Later in 2019, Tesla awarded a car and $375,000 to [ethical hackers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_hat_(computer_security) "White hat (computer security)") during a [Pwn2Own](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pwn2Own "Pwn2Own") Model 3 hacking event.[&#8202;](#ref-back-554-0)[\[553\]](#refnote-554)

In June 2022, Martin Herfurt, a security researcher in Austria, discovered that changes made to make Tesla vehicles easier to start with [NFC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-field_communication "Near-field communication") cards also allowed for pairing new keys to the vehicle, allowing an attacker to enroll their keys to a vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-555-0)[\[554\]](#refnote-555)

### Phantom braking

In February 2022, Tesla drivers reported a surge in "phantom braking" events when using Tesla Autopilot, which coincides with the automaker's removal of radar as a supplemental sensor in May 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-556-0)[\[555\]](#refnote-556) In response, NHTSA opened an investigation the same month.[&#8202;](#ref-back-557-0)[\[556\]](#refnote-557) In August 2022, a consumer class action was filed alleging that the Autopilot system in Tesla cars "contains a hazardous defect which causes the vehicle to suddenly and unintentionally brake".[&#8202;](#ref-back-558-0)[\[557\]](#refnote-558)[&#8202;](#ref-back-559-0)[\[558\]](#refnote-559) In November 2024, Tesla failed to persuade a [US District Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_District_Judge "US District Judge") to dismiss the lawsuit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[559\]](#refnote-:0) The next court date is January 7, 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[559\]](#refnote-:0)

In May 2023, German business newspaper _[Handelsblatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handelsblatt "Handelsblatt")_ published a series of articles based on a trove of internal Tesla data submitted to them from informants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-561-0)[\[560\]](#refnote-561) The 100 gigabytes of data "contain\[&#8202;](#ref-back-562-0)[ed\] over 1,000 accident reports involving phantom braking or unintended acceleration" as well as complaints about Tesla Autopilot.[\[561\]](#refnote-562) [Dutch authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Data_Protection_Authority "Dutch Data Protection Authority") responded by saying they were investigating the company for possible data privacy violations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-563-0)[\[562\]](#refnote-563)

In February 2025, a German court declared Tesla Autopilot defective for normal use due to phantom braking issues.[&#8202;](#ref-back-564-0)[\[563\]](#refnote-564)

In February 2025, a group of Australian buyers sued Tesla with a [class action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_action "Class action") over multiple issues like battery range, driving assistance, and phantom braking.[&#8202;](#ref-back-565-0)[\[564\]](#refnote-565)

In July 2026, the [NHTSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHTSA "NHTSA") ended its Tesla phantom braking probe as customer complaints fell.[&#8202;](#ref-back-566-0)[\[565\]](#refnote-566)

### Driving range performance

Tesla has received thousands of complaints from owners that the driving ranges of their vehicles did not meet the ranges advertised by Tesla or the projections of in-dash range meters. When service centers were overwhelmed with appointments to take care of these issues, Tesla established a diversion team to cancel as many appointments as possible. Customers were told that remote diagnostics had determined there was no problem, and their appointments were canceled. The company has been fined by South Korean regulators for its exaggerated range estimates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-567-0)[\[566\]](#refnote-567)

### Suspension failure risks

In 2026, the [National Highway Traffic Safety Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Highway_Traffic_Safety_Administration "National Highway Traffic Safety Administration") opened a preliminary investigation into Tesla over complaints of a suspension failure risk in 2018–2020 Model 3 and 2021–2023 Model Y vehicles. At the time, no crashes, injuries or fatalities had been reported.[&#8202;](#ref-back-568-0)[\[567\]](#refnote-568)

## Vehicle sales

In 2024, Tesla ranked as the world's best-selling [battery electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battery_electric_vehicle "Battery electric vehicle") passenger car manufacturer, with a market share of 17.6%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-569-0)[\[568\]](#refnote-569) Tesla reported 2023 vehicle deliveries of 1.8 million units, up 38% from 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-570-0)[\[569\]](#refnote-570)[&#8202;](#ref-back-571-0)[\[570\]](#refnote-571) In March 2024, Tesla produced its six millionth car.[&#8202;](#ref-back-572-0)[\[571\]](#refnote-572) In Q4 2023, Tesla's Chinese competitor [BYD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BYD_Auto "BYD Auto") took over the top spot for EVs shipped, but Tesla regained the title in Q1 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-573-0)[\[572\]](#refnote-573) On January 2, 2026, BYD became the bestselling electric vehicle maker across the globe and surpassed Tesla as the largest EV maker.[&#8202;](#ref-back-574-0)[\[573\]](#refnote-574)

Tesla sells the most vehicles in the US (674,000+ in 2023) and has received $11.4 billion in regulatory credits from federal and state governments. The federal government previously provided a $7,500 rebate to customers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-575-0)[\[574\]](#refnote-575)[&#8202;](#ref-back-576-0)[\[575\]](#refnote-576)[&#8202;](#ref-back-577-0)[\[576\]](#refnote-577) Tesla received around $426 million from the Chinese government for cars manufactured at its Shanghai facility.[&#8202;](#ref-back-578-0)[\[577\]](#refnote-578)
Germany was Tesla's third-largest market in 2023, selling 63,682 units. Germany ended its EV subsidy program in December 2023. Under the program, a €6,750 discount was provided with Tesla paying €2,250. Tesla announced it would cover the entire discount going forward.[&#8202;](#ref-back-579-0)[\[578\]](#refnote-579) In 2023, over 63,000 Teslas were sold in France with customers receiving up to €7,000 via the bonus écologique. The government decreased the budget for EV aid from €1.5 billion to €700 million in 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-580-0)[\[579\]](#refnote-580)
Canada was in Tesla's top five markets in 2023 with over 52,000 vehicles sold. In 2025, Tesla was accused of having "a run on the bank" when it sold a historic 8,600 vehicles in 72 hours before [Canada's EV rebate program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_incentives_for_plug-in_electric_vehicles#Canada "Government incentives for plug-in electric vehicles")'s funds were depleted. Tesla claimed 4,000 vehicles alone were sold over one weekend in Quebec City.[&#8202;](#ref-back-581-0)[\[580\]](#refnote-581)[&#8202;](#ref-back-582-0)[\[581\]](#refnote-582) The Government of Canada announced on March 25, 2025, that it had suspended the rebates and was investigating. The government also declared Tesla would be ineligible for any future rebates as long as the US tariffs on Canadian products continued.[&#8202;](#ref-back-583-0)[\[582\]](#refnote-583)
Musk has stated he is against government subsidies but will take them if they are available.[&#8202;](#ref-back-584-0)[\[583\]](#refnote-584)

Sales have been negatively impacted by Musk's actions during the [second Trump presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Trump_presidency "Second Trump presidency").[&#8202;](#ref-back-585-0)[\[584\]](#refnote-585)[&#8202;](#ref-back-586-0)[\[585\]](#refnote-586) Tesla sales have decreased worldwide (the most in countries like France, Germany, the UK, Sweden, Norway, and the Netherlands)[&#8202;](#ref-back-587-0)[\[586\]](#refnote-587)[&#8202;](#ref-back-588-0)[\[587\]](#refnote-588) with his vocal support for far-right parties such as [Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK "Reform UK")[&#8202;](#ref-back-589-0)[\[588\]](#refnote-589) and [AfD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AfD "AfD")[&#8202;](#ref-back-590-0)[\[589\]](#refnote-590) in Germany along with his [Nazi salute controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk_salute_controversy "Elon Musk salute controversy") leading to boycotts of his products and Tesla vehicles being nicknamed **Swasticars**, a [portmanteau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portmanteau "Portmanteau") of _[swastika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swastika "Swastika")_ and _car_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-597-0)[\[596\]](#refnote-597) German sales fell 76% in February 2025 compared to February 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-598-0)[\[597\]](#refnote-598)[&#8202;](#ref-back-599-0)[\[598\]](#refnote-599)

Tesla's shares dropped over 9 percent after its sales in the EU and UK declined by nearly half in January 2025, bringing its valuation below $1 trillion for the first time since November 2024. While European electric car sales surged, Tesla struggled against rising competition, particularly from Chinese automakers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-600-0)[\[599\]](#refnote-600) In May 2025, it was reported that the company's sales in Europe declined every month since the start of the year, including in April where sales dropped 49 percent to 7,261 from 14,228 in April 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[600\]](#refnote-:1) As of May 2025, the company has a 0.7 percent market share in Europe, down from 1.3 percent a year ago.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[600\]](#refnote-:1)

Tesla deliveries vary significantly by month due to regional issues such as availability of [car carriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car_carrier "Car carrier") and registration. On March 9, 2020, the company produced its 1millionth electric car, becoming the first [auto manufacturer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto_manufacturer "Auto manufacturer") to achieve such a milestone.[&#8202;](#ref-back-1million-0)[\[601\]](#refnote-1million) In the third quarter of 2021, Tesla sold its 2 millionth electric car, becoming the first auto manufacturer to achieve such a milestone.[&#8202;](#ref-back-603-0)[\[602\]](#refnote-603) In the first quarter of 2023, the Model Y became the world's best-selling car, surpassing the Toyota Corolla.[&#8202;](#ref-back-604-0)[\[603\]](#refnote-604)

## Finances

Tesla financial performance (in billion USD)

For the fiscal (and calendar) year 2021, Tesla reported a net income of $5.52billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tesla4Q2021final-0)[\[604\]](#refnote-Tesla4Q2021final) The annual revenue was $53.8billion, an increase of 71% over the previous fiscal year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tesla4Q2021final-1)[\[604\]](#refnote-Tesla4Q2021final)

| Business | Sales in billion $ | Share |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Automotive | 87.6 | 89.7% |
| Energy Generation and Storage | 10.1 | 10.3% |

Sales by business (2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[605\]](#refnote-:5)

| Region | Sales in billion $ | Share |
| --- | --- | --- |
| United States | 47.7 | 48.9% |
| Other countries | 29.0 | 29.7% |
| China | 20.9 | 21.4% |

Sales by region (2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-1)[\[605\]](#refnote-:5)

Of the revenue number in 2021, $314million came from selling regulatory credits to other automakers to meet government pollution standards. That number has been a smaller percentage of revenue for multiple quarters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tesla4Q2021final-2)[\[604\]](#refnote-Tesla4Q2021final)

Tesla ended 2021 with $17.6 billion of cash on hand, down $1.8 billion from the end of 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-10K_2021-3)[\[183\]](#refnote-10K_2021):31

In February 2021, a [10-K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Form_10-K "Form 10-K") filing revealed that Tesla had invested some $1.5billion in the cryptocurrency bitcoin, and the company indicated it would soon accept bitcoin as a form of payment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bb20210208-1)[\[88\]](#refnote-bb20210208) Critics then pointed out how investing in cryptocurrency can run counter to Tesla's environmental goals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-607-0)[\[606\]](#refnote-607)[&#8202;](#ref-back-608-0)[\[607\]](#refnote-608) Tesla made more profit from the 2021 investment than the profit from selling cars in 2020, due to the bitcoin price increase after the investment was announced.[&#8202;](#ref-back-609-0)[\[608\]](#refnote-609)[&#8202;](#ref-back-610-0)[\[609\]](#refnote-610)

The quarter ending June 2021 was the first time Tesla made a profit independent of bitcoin and regulatory credits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-611-0)[\[610\]](#refnote-611) Between 2015 and 2024, a third of Tesla's profits came from government climate credits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-612-0)[\[611\]](#refnote-612)

### Board of directors

Tesla has received criticism that its board lacks enough independent directors. In an April 2017 public letter, a group of influential Tesla investors, including the [California State Teachers' Retirement System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_State_Teachers'_Retirement_System "California State Teachers' Retirement System"), asked Tesla to add two new independent directors to its board "who do not have any ties" with Musk,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent-0)[\[617\]](#refnote-Independent) writing that "five of six current non-executive directors have professional or personal ties to Mr. Musk that could put at risk their ability to exercise independent judgement."[&#8202;](#ref-back-USA-Today-0)[\[618\]](#refnote-USA-Today) Tesla's directors at the time included Brad Buss, who was chief financial officer at SolarCity; [Steve Jurvetson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jurvetson "Steve Jurvetson"), a venture capitalist who also sits on the board of SpaceX;[&#8202;](#ref-back-BI_BOD_2018-0)[\[619\]](#refnote-BI_BOD_2018) Elon Musk's brother, [Kimbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimbal_Musk "Kimbal Musk"); and Ira Ehrenpreis and [Antonio Gracias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Gracias "Antonio Gracias"), both of whom also invested in SpaceX.[&#8202;](#ref-back-621-0)[\[620\]](#refnote-621) The letter called for a more independent board that could put a check on [groupthink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groupthink "Groupthink").[&#8202;](#ref-back-USA-Today-1)[\[618\]](#refnote-USA-Today) At first Musk responded on Twitter, writing that the investors "should buy Ford stock" because "their governance is amazing."[&#8202;](#ref-back-USA-Today-2)[\[618\]](#refnote-USA-Today) Two days later, he promised he would add two independent board members;[&#8202;](#ref-back-622-0)[\[621\]](#refnote-622) [Kathleen Wilson-Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathleen_Wilson-Thompson "Kathleen Wilson-Thompson") and [Larry Ellison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Ellison "Larry Ellison") were added at the end of 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-623-0)[\[622\]](#refnote-623) Ellison stepped down in August 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-624-0)[\[623\]](#refnote-624) Former Tesla CTO [J. B. Straubel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._Straubel "J. B. Straubel"), who left the company in 2019, was elected to the board in 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jb-board-0)[\[624\]](#refnote-jb-board)

Another criticism of the board composition is that most of the independent directors lack automotive industry experience.[&#8202;](#ref-back-626-0)[\[625\]](#refnote-626) The exception is Robyn Denholm, who had finance and corporate reporting roles at [Toyota Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Australia "Toyota Australia") from 1989 to 1996.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cleantech_denholm_2018-0)[\[626\]](#refnote-cleantech_denholm_2018)

Other previous board members include businessman [Steve Westly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Westly "Steve Westly"); Daimler executive Herbert Kohler;[&#8202;](#ref-back-TN_BOD_2012-1)[\[309\]](#refnote-TN_BOD_2012) CEO and Chairman of [Johnson Publishing Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Publishing_Company "Johnson Publishing Company") Linda Johnson Rice;[&#8202;](#ref-back-GTM_BOD_2019-0)[\[627\]](#refnote-GTM_BOD_2019) and [United Nations Special Envoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Envoy_of_the_Secretary-General "Special Envoy of the Secretary-General") on Innovative Finance and Sustainable Investments [Hiromichi Mizuno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiromichi_Mizuno "Hiromichi Mizuno").[&#8202;](#ref-back-629-0)[\[628\]](#refnote-629)[&#8202;](#ref-back-630-0)[\[629\]](#refnote-630)

As of December31,2025[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Tesla,_Inc.&action=edit), the board members are:[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[630\]](#refnote-:10)

### Taxes

From 2007 to 2025, except for 2023, Tesla has declared owing no taxes to the US government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[640\]](#refnote-:2) Tesla likely saved $400 million in US taxes over the years by shifting $18 billion in profits to Singapore and the Netherlands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[640\]](#refnote-:2)

Tesla was founded not by Elon Musk, but rather by Martin Eberhard and Marc Tarpenning in July 2003. The two bootstrapped the fledgling auto company until [Elon Musk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk "Elon Musk") led the company's US$7.5million Series A financing round in February 2004.
